Hi, doctor. Thank you so much for seeing me. Um, I hope I'm not taking up time from someone who needs it more, it's really just... well, my wife thought it would be a good idea for me to come in, and I want to make sure I'm doing the right thing.

It's just... \n\nI guess I don't really have a specific, concrete example to give you right now. It's more of a feeling, you know? Like, the timing of things, or just the way she phrases certain texts when she says she's going to be late. But when I try to pin it down and explain it, it just sounds like... well, like normal office stuff, just like she says. \n\nMaybe I am just overthinking it all. I don't want to waste your time with things that might just be in my head. Does that make sense? I mean, is it normal for people to come in here and not really be able to explain exactly what's bothering them? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the information you need.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that your wife is intentionally sending hidden messages to someone else through her work communications, even though there may be no clear proof?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... no, not to someone else. I'm sorry if I gave that impression, I definitely don't want to make it sound like I think she's having an affair or doing anything terrible like that. She's incredibly loyal, she really is. \n\nIt's actually... well, I feel like the messages are meant for me. I know I sort of touched on this a moment ago, and I hope it's okay if I try to explain it again. It's just that... I know she says the late meetings and the little tweaks in her work messages are just normal office stuff. I really do try to accept that, because I want to be a good, supportive husband. But for the past year, they’ve just lined up way too precisely to feel like an accident. It feels like there’s a private signal in them, meant to tell me something she just won’t say directly to my face. \n\nDoes that answer what you were asking? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right kind of information. I know it probably sounds a bit silly or overly sensitive when I say it out loud like that.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever felt that certain things in your environment, like messages or events, have a special meaning specifically for you that others don't seem to notice?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 48 / teaching_assistant / graduate\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Delusions\nmanifestation: \"I know my wife says the late meetings and little changes in her work messages are just normal office stuff, but for the past year they’ve lined up too precisely to feel accidental, like there’s a private signal in them meant to tell me something she won’t say directly.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, um, yes, I suppose I have. I know we just talked about this, and I hope I'm not repeating myself too much—I just want to make sure I'm answering your question the way you need me to. 

It really goes back to what I was saying about my wife's messages. I mean, I know she insists that the late meetings and those little changes in her texts are just normal office stuff. And I try to believe her, I really do, because I don't want to be difficult or demanding. But... for the past year, it just feels like they line up too precisely, you know? It doesn't feel accidental. It feels like there’s a private signal in them, just for me. Like she's trying to tell me something she won't say directly. 

I know other people wouldn't look at a text about a budget meeting and think it meant anything else. And I really hope I don't sound completely unreasonable saying this. Is it okay that I feel this way? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the kind of examples you're looking for.

I mean, is it normal for people to come in here and not really be able to explain exactly what's bothering them? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the information you need.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that your wife is intentionally sending hidden messages to someone else through her work communications, even though there may be no clear proof?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... no, not to someone else. I'm sorry if I gave that impression, I definitely don't want to make it sound like I think she's having an affair or doing anything terrible like that. She's incredibly loyal, she really is. \n\nIt's actually... well, I feel like the messages are meant for me. I know I sort of touched on this a moment ago, and I hope it's okay if I try to explain it again. It's just that... I know she says the late meetings and the little tweaks in her work messages are just normal office stuff. I really do try to accept that, because I want to be a good, supportive husband. But for the past year, they’ve just lined up way too precisely to feel like an accident. It feels like there’s a private signal in them, meant to tell me something she just won’t say directly to my face. \n\nDoes that answer what you were asking? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right kind of information. I know it probably sounds a bit silly or overly sensitive when I say it out loud like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that certain things in your environment, like messages or events, have a special meaning specifically for you that others don't seem to notice?\n\nPatient: Well, um, yes, I suppose I have. I know we just talked about this, and I hope I'm not repeating myself too much—I just want to make sure I'm answering your question the way you need me to. \n\nIt really goes back to what I was saying about my wife's messages. I mean, I know she insists that the late meetings and those little changes in her texts are just normal office stuff. And I try to believe her, I really do, because I don't want to be difficult or demanding. But... for the past year, it just feels like they line up too precisely, you know? It doesn't feel accidental. It feels like there’s a private signal in them, just for me. Like she's trying to tell me something she won't say directly. \n\nI know other people wouldn't look at a text about a budget meeting and think it meant anything else. And I really hope I don't sound completely unreasonable saying this. Is it okay that I feel this way? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the kind of examples you're looking for.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that things happening around you—like something on TV, a song playing, or even strangers’ conversations—were directed at you or carried a hidden message meant only for you to understand?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... no. I really wouldn't want you to think I'm completely losing touch with reality or anything like that. \n\nI know the thing with my wife's texts might sound a little out there, and I appreciate you listening to me about it, but it really is just limited to her and those specific work messages. Everyone at work is just... well, they're just doing their jobs. \n\nIt really is just my wife's messages. Well, um... I suppose this brings me right back to what we were just talking about. I'm sorry to keep repeating myself, I really hope I don't sound like a broken record. Is it alright if I use the same example? 

Because, yes, that's exactly how I feel about my wife's situation. She tells me constantly that there's nothing going on and that I don't have any evidence. She swears that the late meetings and those little changes in her work messages are just normal office stuff. And I really, really try to see it her way, because I want to be supportive and I don't want to cause trouble at home.

I know it probably sounds stubborn of me to hold onto that when she says otherwise, and I really don't want to come across as unreasonable. I just... I can't help but feel certain about it. Does that make sense? I hope that's the kind of answer you were looking for.

I know it probably sounds a bit silly or overly sensitive when I say it out loud like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that certain things in your environment, like messages or events, have a special meaning specifically for you that others don't seem to notice?\n\nPatient: Well, um, yes, I suppose I have. But when I try to pin it down and explain it, it just sounds like... well, like normal office stuff, just like she says. \n\nMaybe I am just overthinking it all. I don't want to waste your time with things that might just be in my head. Does that make sense? I mean, is it normal for people to come in here and not really be able to explain exactly what's bothering them? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the information you need.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that your wife is intentionally sending hidden messages to someone else through her work communications, even though there may be no clear proof?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... no, not to someone else. I'm sorry if I gave that impression, I definitely don't want to make it sound like I think she's having an affair or doing anything terrible like that. She's incredibly loyal, she really is. \n\nIt's actually... well, I feel like the messages are meant for me. I know I sort of touched on this a moment ago, and I hope it's okay if I try to explain it again. It's just that... I know she says the late meetings and the little tweaks in her work messages are just normal office stuff. I really do try to accept that, because I want to be a good, supportive husband. But for the past year, they’ve just lined up way too precisely to feel like an accident. It feels like there’s a private signal in them, meant to tell me something she just won’t say directly to my face. \n\nDoes that answer what you were asking? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right kind of information. I know it probably sounds a bit silly or overly sensitive when I say it out loud like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that certain things in your environment, like messages or events, have a special meaning specifically for you that others don't seem to notice?\n\nPatient: Well, um, yes, I suppose I have. I know we just talked about this, and I hope I'm not repeating myself too much—I just want to make sure I'm answering your question the way you need me to. \n\nIt really goes back to what I was saying about my wife's messages. I mean, I know she insists that the late meetings and those little changes in her texts are just normal office stuff. And I try to believe her, I really do, because I don't want to be difficult or demanding. But... for the past year, it just feels like they line up too precisely, you know? It doesn't feel accidental. It feels like there’s a private signal in them, just for me. Like she's trying to tell me something she won't say directly. \n\nI know other people wouldn't look at a text about a budget meeting and think it meant anything else. And I really hope I don't sound completely unreasonable saying this. Is it okay that I feel this way? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the kind of examples you're looking for.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that things happening around you—like something on TV, a song playing, or even strangers’ conversations—were directed at you or carried a hidden message meant only for you to understand?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... no. No, definitely not. \n\nI mean, I watch TV and listen to the radio just like anyone else, but... no, I've never felt like a news anchor or a song on the radio was talking to me. That would be... well, I know that would be a bit extreme, wouldn't it? I really wouldn't want you to think I'm completely losing touch with reality or anything like that. \n\nI know the thing with my wife's texts might sound a little out there, and I appreciate you listening to me about it, but it really is just limited to her and those specific work messages. Strangers on the street or people on TV are just going about their day. \n\nI hope that's okay? I just want to make sure I'm being helpful and giving you an accurate picture of what's going on. Did I answer that the way you needed me to?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that people you know, like friends or coworkers, were also sending you hidden messages in their everyday communication, even if they didn’t realize it?\n\nPatient: Oh, no. No, I haven't noticed anything like that with anyone else. \n\nI really try to keep a good relationship with my coworkers and the students I help out at the university. I'd hate to think they were doing anything behind my back, or that I was misreading their emails. Everyone at work is just... well, they're just doing their jobs. \n\nIt really is just my wife's messages. I know it probably seems strange that I'm so focused on her texts and not anyone else's, but she's the person I'm closest to, you know? I hope it doesn't make me sound paranoid or anything that I'm so zeroed in on her. I really am quite rational when it comes to the rest of my life. \n\nIs that the kind of answer you were looking for? I just want to be sure I'm giving you the right information and not making things seem worse than they are.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt certain about something being true, even when others told you there was no evidence to support it?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I suppose this brings me right back to what we were just talking about. I'm sorry to keep repeating myself, I really hope I don't sound like a broken record. Is it alright if I use the same example? \n\nBecause, yes, that's exactly how I feel about my wife's situation. She tells me constantly that there's nothing going on and that I don't have any evidence. She swears that the late meetings and those little changes in her work messages are just normal office stuff. And I really, really try to see it her way, because I want to be supportive and I don't want to cause trouble at home. \n\nBut... I just can't shake how certain I am about it. For the past year, everything has just lined up too precisely to feel accidental. Even if she says there's no proof, I just know in my gut that there’s a private signal in those messages meant to tell me something she won’t say directly. \n\nI know it probably sounds stubborn of me to hold onto that when she says otherwise, and I really don't want to come across as unreasonable. I just... I can't help but feel certain about it. Does that make sense? I hope that's the kind of answer you were looking for.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had strong beliefs about other specific things—like your health, your abilities, or something else—that others said weren't true, even though you felt certain they were?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... no. Nothing like that. \n\nI mean, I'm pretty healthy, thankfully. I try to take good care of myself, you know, eat right, get some exercise when I can. I definitely don't have any strange ideas about my body or some hidden illness or anything. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, no, definitely not. 

I mean, I really try to be open-minded about things. If someone tells me I'm wrong about something, I'm usually the first to step back and re-evaluate, you know? I don't like to be difficult or cause arguments. I'm actually a pretty agreeable person, most of the time.

Oh, goodness, no. No, nothing like that at all.

I mean, is it normal for people to come in here and not really be able to explain exactly what's bothering them? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the information you need.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that your wife is intentionally sending hidden messages to someone else through her work communications, even though there may be no clear proof?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... no, not to someone else. I'm sorry if I gave that impression, I definitely don't want to make it sound like I think she's having an affair or doing anything terrible like that. She's incredibly loyal, she really is. \n\nIt's actually... well, I feel like the messages are meant for me. I know I sort of touched on this a moment ago, and I hope it's okay if I try to explain it again. It's just that... I know she says the late meetings and the little tweaks in her work messages are just normal office stuff. I really do try to accept that, because I want to be a good, supportive husband. But for the past year, they’ve just lined up way too precisely to feel like an accident. It feels like there’s a private signal in them, meant to tell me something she just won’t say directly to my face. \n\nDoes that answer what you were asking? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right kind of information. I know it probably sounds a bit silly or overly sensitive when I say it out loud like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that certain things in your environment, like messages or events, have a special meaning specifically for you that others don't seem to notice?\n\nPatient: Well, um, yes, I suppose I have. I know we just talked about this, and I hope I'm not repeating myself too much—I just want to make sure I'm answering your question the way you need me to. \n\nIt really goes back to what I was saying about my wife's messages. I mean, I know she insists that the late meetings and those little changes in her texts are just normal office stuff. And I try to believe her, I really do, because I don't want to be difficult or demanding. But... for the past year, it just feels like they line up too precisely, you know? It doesn't feel accidental. It feels like there’s a private signal in them, just for me. Like she's trying to tell me something she won't say directly. \n\nI know other people wouldn't look at a text about a budget meeting and think it meant anything else. And I really hope I don't sound completely unreasonable saying this. Is it okay that I feel this way? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the kind of examples you're looking for.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that things happening around you—like something on TV, a song playing, or even strangers’ conversations—were directed at you or carried a hidden message meant only for you to understand?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... no. No, definitely not. \n\nI mean, I watch TV and listen to the radio just like anyone else, but... no, I've never felt like a news anchor or a song on the radio was talking to me. That would be... well, I know that would be a bit extreme, wouldn't it? I really wouldn't want you to think I'm completely losing touch with reality or anything like that. \n\nI know the thing with my wife's texts might sound a little out there, and I appreciate you listening to me about it, but it really is just limited to her and those specific work messages. Strangers on the street or people on TV are just going about their day. \n\nI hope that's okay? I just want to make sure I'm being helpful and giving you an accurate picture of what's going on. Did I answer that the way you needed me to?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that people you know, like friends or coworkers, were also sending you hidden messages in their everyday communication, even if they didn’t realize it?\n\nPatient: Oh, no. No, I haven't noticed anything like that with anyone else. \n\nI really try to keep a good relationship with my coworkers and the students I help out at the university. I'd hate to think they were doing anything behind my back, or that I was misreading their emails. Everyone at work is just... well, they're just doing their jobs. \n\nIt really is just my wife's messages. I know it probably seems strange that I'm so focused on her texts and not anyone else's, but she's the person I'm closest to, you know? I hope it doesn't make me sound paranoid or anything that I'm so zeroed in on her. I really am quite rational when it comes to the rest of my life. \n\nIs that the kind of answer you were looking for? I just want to be sure I'm giving you the right information and not making things seem worse than they are.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt certain about something being true, even when others told you there was no evidence to support it?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I suppose this brings me right back to what we were just talking about. I'm sorry to keep repeating myself, I really hope I don't sound like a broken record. Is it alright if I use the same example? \n\nBecause, yes, that's exactly how I feel about my wife's situation. She tells me constantly that there's nothing going on and that I don't have any evidence. She swears that the late meetings and those little changes in her work messages are just normal office stuff. And I really, really try to see it her way, because I want to be supportive and I don't want to cause trouble at home. \n\nBut... I just can't shake how certain I am about it. For the past year, everything has just lined up too precisely to feel accidental. Even if she says there's no proof, I just know in my gut that there’s a private signal in those messages meant to tell me something she won’t say directly. \n\nI know it probably sounds stubborn of me to hold onto that when she says otherwise, and I really don't want to come across as unreasonable. I just... I can't help but feel certain about it. Does that make sense? I hope that's the kind of answer you were looking for.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had strong beliefs about other specific things—like your health, your abilities, or something else—that others said weren't true, even though you felt certain they were?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... no. Nothing like that. \n\nI mean, I'm pretty healthy, thankfully. I try to take good care of myself, you know, eat right, get some exercise when I can. I definitely don't have any strange ideas about my body or some hidden illness or anything. I know some people get really anxious about their health and go to the doctor for every little ache and pain, but I'm really not like that. I wouldn't want you to think I'm a hypochondriac. \n\nAnd as for my abilities... well, I'm just a teaching assistant. I do my best to help the students and support the professors, but I certainly don't think I'm a genius or have some special talent that no one else can see. I know my place, and I'm perfectly happy with it. I'm just a regular guy, really. \n\nSo, no, I haven't had any other strong beliefs like that. It really is just this one specific thing with my wife. I hope that's alright? I want to make sure I'm giving you the right picture of everything. Am I doing okay with these questions?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a belief that you were completely certain about for a month or more, even when others said it wasn’t true or there was no proof to support it—other than the situation with your wife’s messages?\n\nPatient: No, no, definitely not. \n\nI mean, I really try to be open-minded about things. If someone tells me I'm wrong about something, I'm usually the first to step back and re-evaluate, you know? I don't like to be difficult or cause arguments. I'm actually a pretty agreeable person, most of the time. \n\nSo, outside of... well, outside of the situation at home with the messages... I haven't had anything like that. No other big beliefs that people told me were wrong. I really try to stay grounded in reality with everything else. I pay my bills, I show up to work, I grade the papers... just normal, everyday stuff. \n\nI hope it doesn't seem like I'm hiding anything from you. I just really want to assure you that this—this thing with my wife—is the only thing. It's just this one isolated issue. Does that help clarify things? I really want to make sure I'm giving you exactly what you need for your notes.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that your actions, thoughts, or feelings were being controlled by something outside of yourself, like a force or power that you couldn't see?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. No, nothing like that at all. \n\nI mean, I'm fully in control of myself. I make my own decisions, I do my own work... I definitely don't feel like anything is, you know, making me do things or putting thoughts in my head. That sounds really frightening, to be honest. I can't imagine how awful it would be to feel like you weren't in charge of your own mind. \n\nI'm just a very normal, predictable guy. I wake up, I go to the university, I come home. I promise I don't have any strange experiences like that. I really hope I'm not giving you the impression that I'm... well, you know, completely out of touch with reality. I know the thing with my wife is a lot, but I really am very grounded otherwise. \n\nIs that the right kind of answer? I just want to make sure you know I'm not experiencing anything that extreme. I'm doing okay, right?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had these kinds of strong beliefs when you were feeling down, hopeless, or especially stressed, or do they happen even when your mood is relatively okay?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, it's not... it's not tied to my mood at all. \n\nI mean, I don't really get depressed or down like that anyway. I try to keep a positive outlook, you know? Even when things are stressful at the university with grading or midterms, I manage it pretty well. I'm not the kind of person who falls apart under pressure or gets hopeless. I always try to look on the bright side.\n\nSo, the thing with my wife... it just happens whenever. It doesn't matter if I'm having a great day or a busy day, it's just... there. Because it's real, you know? It's not because I'm feeling stressed or sad and making things up in my head. I'm actually a very even-keeled person. \n\nI hope that makes sense? I just want to be clear that my mood is generally really stable. I'm not letting stress get the better of me. Does that sound like a normal response? I want to make sure I'm giving you the right information.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any beliefs that others considered strange or untrue, even when there was no doubt in your mind that they were completely accurate—other than the messages from your wife?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... no, nothing like that. I know we touched on something kind of similar a few minutes ago, and I really don't want to sound like a broken record for you, but... no. Just the situation with my wife. \n\nI really pride myself on being a logical person, you know? With my work as a TA, I have to be objective and rational for the students. So if someone told me I was holding onto some strange or untrue belief, I'd... well, I'd be pretty embarrassed, honestly. I would want to correct it right away so people wouldn't think poorly of me. I really care about how I come across to my colleagues and, well, to you, too.\n\nI hope I'm being helpful here. I know you have to ask all these different variations of questions to do your job properly, and I just want to make sure I'm giving you exactly what you need for your assessment. Am I giving you enough detail? I can try to elaborate more if you need me to, I just really don't have any other unusual ideas like that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I just want to make sure I'm giving you the kind of examples you're looking for.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that things happening around you—like something on TV, a song playing, or even strangers’ conversations—were directed at you or carried a hidden message meant only for you to understand?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... no. No, definitely not. \n\nI mean, I watch TV and listen to the radio just like anyone else, but... no, I've never felt like a news anchor or a song on the radio was talking to me. That would be... well, I know that would be a bit extreme, wouldn't it? I really wouldn't want you to think I'm completely losing touch with reality or anything like that. \n\nI know the thing with my wife's texts might sound a little out there, and I appreciate you listening to me about it, but it really is just limited to her and those specific work messages. Strangers on the street or people on TV are just going about their day. \n\nI hope that's okay? I just want to make sure I'm being helpful and giving you an accurate picture of what's going on. Did I answer that the way you needed me to?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that people you know, like friends or coworkers, were also sending you hidden messages in their everyday communication, even if they didn’t realize it?\n\nPatient: Oh, no. No, I haven't noticed anything like that with anyone else. \n\nI really try to keep a good relationship with my coworkers and the students I help out at the university. I'd hate to think they were doing anything behind my back, or that I was misreading their emails. Everyone at work is just... well, they're just doing their jobs. \n\nIt really is just my wife's messages. I know it probably seems strange that I'm so focused on her texts and not anyone else's, but she's the person I'm closest to, you know? I hope it doesn't make me sound paranoid or anything that I'm so zeroed in on her. I really am quite rational when it comes to the rest of my life. \n\nIs that the kind of answer you were looking for? I just want to be sure I'm giving you the right information and not making things seem worse than they are.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt certain about something being true, even when others told you there was no evidence to support it?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I suppose this brings me right back to what we were just talking about. I'm sorry to keep repeating myself, I really hope I don't sound like a broken record. Is it alright if I use the same example? \n\nBecause, yes, that's exactly how I feel about my wife's situation. She tells me constantly that there's nothing going on and that I don't have any evidence. She swears that the late meetings and those little changes in her work messages are just normal office stuff. And I really, really try to see it her way, because I want to be supportive and I don't want to cause trouble at home. \n\nBut... I just can't shake how certain I am about it. For the past year, everything has just lined up too precisely to feel accidental. Even if she says there's no proof, I just know in my gut that there’s a private signal in those messages meant to tell me something she won’t say directly. \n\nI know it probably sounds stubborn of me to hold onto that when she says otherwise, and I really don't want to come across as unreasonable. I just... I can't help but feel certain about it. Does that make sense? I hope that's the kind of answer you were looking for.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had strong beliefs about other specific things—like your health, your abilities, or something else—that others said weren't true, even though you felt certain they were?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... no. Nothing like that. \n\nI mean, I'm pretty healthy, thankfully. I try to take good care of myself, you know, eat right, get some exercise when I can. I definitely don't have any strange ideas about my body or some hidden illness or anything. I know some people get really anxious about their health and go to the doctor for every little ache and pain, but I'm really not like that. I wouldn't want you to think I'm a hypochondriac. \n\nAnd as for my abilities... well, I'm just a teaching assistant. I do my best to help the students and support the professors, but I certainly don't think I'm a genius or have some special talent that no one else can see. I know my place, and I'm perfectly happy with it. I'm just a regular guy, really. \n\nSo, no, I haven't had any other strong beliefs like that. It really is just this one specific thing with my wife. I hope that's alright? I want to make sure I'm giving you the right picture of everything. Am I doing okay with these questions?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a belief that you were completely certain about for a month or more, even when others said it wasn’t true or there was no proof to support it—other than the situation with your wife’s messages?\n\nPatient: No, no, definitely not. \n\nI mean, I really try to be open-minded about things. If someone tells me I'm wrong about something, I'm usually the first to step back and re-evaluate, you know? I don't like to be difficult or cause arguments. I'm actually a pretty agreeable person, most of the time. \n\nSo, outside of... well, outside of the situation at home with the messages... I haven't had anything like that. No other big beliefs that people told me were wrong. I really try to stay grounded in reality with everything else. I pay my bills, I show up to work, I grade the papers... just normal, everyday stuff. \n\nI hope it doesn't seem like I'm hiding anything from you. I just really want to assure you that this—this thing with my wife—is the only thing. It's just this one isolated issue. Does that help clarify things? I really want to make sure I'm giving you exactly what you need for your notes.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that your actions, thoughts, or feelings were being controlled by something outside of yourself, like a force or power that you couldn't see?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. No, nothing like that at all. \n\nI mean, I'm fully in control of myself. I make my own decisions, I do my own work... I definitely don't feel like anything is, you know, making me do things or putting thoughts in my head. That sounds really frightening, to be honest. I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right kind of information. I know it probably sounds a bit silly or overly sensitive when I say it out loud like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that certain things in your environment, like messages or events, have a special meaning specifically for you that others don't seem to notice?\n\nPatient: Well, um, yes, I suppose I have. I know we just talked about this, and I hope I'm not repeating myself too much—I just want to make sure I'm answering your question the way you need me to. \n\nIt really goes back to what I was saying about my wife's messages. I mean, I know she insists that the late meetings and those little changes in her texts are just normal office stuff. And I try to believe her, I really do, because I don't want to be difficult or demanding. But... for the past year, it just feels like they line up too precisely, you know? It doesn't feel accidental. It feels like there’s a private signal in them, just for me. Like she's trying to tell me something she won't say directly. \n\nI know other people wouldn't look at a text about a budget meeting and think it meant anything else. And I really hope I don't sound completely unreasonable saying this. Is it okay that I feel this way? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the kind of examples you're looking for.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that things happening around you—like something on TV, a song playing, or even strangers’ conversations—were directed at you or carried a hidden message meant only for you to understand?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... no. No, definitely not. \n\nI mean, I watch TV and listen to the radio just like anyone else, but... no, I've never felt like a news anchor or a song on the radio was talking to me. That would be... well, I know that would be a bit extreme, wouldn't it? I really wouldn't want you to think I'm completely losing touch with reality or anything like that. \n\nI know the thing with my wife's texts might sound a little out there, and I appreciate you listening to me about it, but it really is just limited to her and those specific work messages. Strangers on the street or people on TV are just going about their day. \n\nI hope that's okay? I just want to make sure I'm being helpful and giving you an accurate picture of what's going on. Did I answer that the way you needed me to?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that people you know, like friends or coworkers, were also sending you hidden messages in their everyday communication, even if they didn’t realize it?\n\nPatient: Oh, no. No, I haven't noticed anything like that with anyone else. \n\nI really try to keep a good relationship with my coworkers and the students I help out at the university. I'd hate to think they were doing anything behind my back, or that I was misreading their emails. Everyone at work is just... well, they're just doing their jobs. \n\nIt really is just my wife's messages. I know it probably seems strange that I'm so focused on her texts and not anyone else's, but she's the person I'm closest to, you know? I hope it doesn't make me sound paranoid or anything that I'm so zeroed in on her. I really am quite rational when it comes to the rest of my life. \n\nIs that the kind of answer you were looking for? I just want to be sure I'm giving you the right information and not making things seem worse than they are.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt certain about something being true, even when others told you there was no evidence to support it?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I suppose this brings me right back to what we were just talking about. I'm sorry to keep repeating myself, I really hope I don't sound like a broken record. Is it alright if I use the same example? \n\nBecause, yes, that's exactly how I feel about my wife's situation. She tells me constantly that there's nothing going on and that I don't have any evidence. She swears that the late meetings and those little changes in her work messages are just normal office stuff. And I really, really try to see it her way, because I want to be supportive and I don't want to cause trouble at home. \n\nBut... I just can't shake how certain I am about it. For the past year, everything has just lined up too precisely to feel accidental. Even if she says there's no proof, I just know in my gut that there’s a private signal in those messages meant to tell me something she won’t say directly. \n\nI know it probably sounds stubborn of me to hold onto that when she says otherwise, and I really don't want to come across as unreasonable. I just... I can't help but feel certain about it. Does that make sense? I hope that's the kind of answer you were looking for.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had strong beliefs about other specific things—like your health, your abilities, or something else—that others said weren't true, even though you felt certain they were?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... no. Nothing like that. \n\nI mean, I'm pretty healthy, thankfully. I try to take good care of myself, you know, eat right, get some exercise when I can. I definitely don't have any strange ideas about my body or some hidden illness or anything. I know some people get really anxious about their health and go to the doctor for every little ache and pain, but I'm really not like that. I wouldn't want you to think I'm a hypochondriac. \n\nAnd as for my abilities... well, I'm just a teaching assistant. I do my best to help the students and support the professors, but I certainly don't think I'm a genius or have some special talent that no one else can see. I know my place, and I'm perfectly happy with it. I'm just a regular guy, really. \n\nSo, no, I haven't had any other strong beliefs like that. It really is just this one specific thing with my wife. I hope that's alright? I want to make sure I'm giving you the right picture of everything. Am I doing okay with these questions?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a belief that you were completely certain about for a month or more, even when others said it wasn’t true or there was no proof to support it—other than the situation with your wife’s messages?\n\nPatient: No, no, definitely not. \n\nI mean, I really try to be open-minded about things. If someone tells me I'm wrong about something, I'm usually the first to step back and re-evaluate, you know? I don't like to be difficult or cause arguments. I'm actually a pretty agreeable person, most of the time. \n\nSo, outside of... well, outside of the situation at home with the messages... I haven't had anything like that. No other big beliefs that people told me were wrong. I really try to stay grounded in reality with everything else. I pay my bills, I show up to work, I grade the papers... just normal, everyday stuff. \n\nI hope it doesn't seem like I'm hiding anything from you. I just really want to assure you that this—this thing with my wife—is the only thing. It's just this one isolated issue. Does that help clarify things? I really want to make sure I'm giving you exactly what you need for your notes.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that your actions, thoughts, or feelings were being controlled by something outside of yourself, like a force or power that you couldn't see?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. No, nothing like that at all. \n\nI mean, I'm fully in control of myself. I make my own decisions, I do my own work... I definitely don't feel like anything is, you know, making me do things or putting thoughts in my head. That sounds really frightening, to be honest. I can't imagine how awful it would be to feel like you weren't in charge of your own mind. \n\nI'm just a very normal, predictable guy. I wake up, I go to the university, I come home. I promise I don't have any strange experiences like that. I really hope I'm not giving you the impression that I'm... well, you know, completely out of touch with reality. I know the thing with my wife is a lot, but I really am very grounded otherwise. \n\nIs that the right kind of answer? I just want to make sure you know I'm not experiencing anything that extreme. I'm doing okay, right?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had these kinds of strong beliefs when you were feeling down, hopeless, or especially stressed, or do they happen even when your mood is relatively okay?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, it's not... it's not tied to my mood at all. \n\nI mean, I don't really get depressed or down like that anyway. I try to keep a positive outlook, you know? Even when things are stressful at the university with grading or midterms, I manage it pretty well. I'm not the kind of person who falls apart under pressure or gets hopeless. I always try to look on the bright side.\n\nSo, the thing with my wife... it just happens whenever. It doesn't matter if I'm having a great day or a busy day, it's just... there. Because it's real, you know? It's not because I'm feeling stressed or sad and making things up in my head. I'm actually a very even-keeled person. \n\nI hope that makes sense? I just want to be clear that my mood is generally really stable. I'm not letting stress get the better of me. Does that sound like a normal response? I want to make sure I'm giving you the right information.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any beliefs that others considered strange or untrue, even when there was no doubt in your mind that they were completely accurate—other than the messages from your wife?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... no, nothing like that. I know we touched on something kind of similar a few minutes ago, and I really don't want to sound like a broken record for you, but... no. Just the situation with my wife. \n\nI really pride myself on being a logical person, you know? With my work as a TA, I have to be objective and rational for the students. So if someone told me I was holding onto some strange or untrue belief, I'd... well, I'd be pretty embarrassed, honestly. I would want to correct it right away so people wouldn't think poorly of me. I really care about how I come across to my colleagues and, well, to you, too.\n\nI hope I'm being helpful here. I know you have to ask all these different variations of questions to do your job properly, and I just want to make sure I'm giving you exactly what you need for your assessment. Am I giving you enough detail? I can try to elaborate more if you need me to, I just really don't have any other unusual ideas like that.\n\nDoctor: Has there ever been a time when you believed something strongly that wasn’t actually true, even though you knew it seemed odd to others—like something happening in your daily life had a special meaning or connection that only you understood?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... no. No, I really haven't. I know we're sort of circling around similar things here, and I hope I'm not being difficult by giving you the same answer again. I just... I don't want you to think I'm the kind of person who reads into things that aren't there. \n\nI'm a very grounded person, really. I don't look for hidden meanings in everyday things, like... I don't know, a song on the radio or a license plate or anything like that. That sounds a bit... well, a bit unhinged, doesn't it? I would hate for anyone to think I was behaving that way. I just stick to the facts in my daily life. I mean, my work requires me to be very analytical, so I try to apply that to everything else, too. \n\nExcept for, you know, the messages from my wife. But that's different because it's... well, it's real. It's not just me making connections out of nowhere. \n\nI hope I'm answering these questions the right way for you? I know you have a thorough process, and I'm really trying to be as cooperative as possible. I just want to make sure you see that I'm generally very reasonable. Is this the kind of information you're looking for?\n\nDoctor: So far, you’ve said that you’re certain your wife is sending you hidden messages through her texts, even though she denies it and there’s no clear proof. How would you react if she came to you with solid evidence that the messages were just work-related—would you be able to accept that, or would you still believe there was a hidden meaning meant for you?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... if she really had solid evidence, I mean, of course I'd want to look at it. I'm a reasonable guy, like I said. I'd never want to just... stubbornly ignore facts. That wouldn't be very fair to her, would it? \n\nBut... the thing is, I know my wife says the late meetings and the little changes in her work messages are just normal office stuff. She tells me that, and I hear her. But for the past year, they've just... they've lined up too precisely to feel accidental, you know? It's like there's a private signal in them meant to tell me something she won't say directly. \n\nSo, if she showed me evidence... I'd try to accept it, I really would. I don't want to be the kind of husband who causes problems or doesn't trust his wife. That sounds awful. I'm really trying to be open-minded about all this. I just want to give you an honest answer. Is it okay that I feel that way?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I mean... I would really try to listen to her. I always want to be fair, and I'd hate for you—or her—to think I'm just being stubborn or ignoring logic. I really pride myself on being reasonable.

I'm a reasonable guy, like I said. I'd never want to just... stubbornly ignore facts. That wouldn't be very fair to her, would it? \n\nBut... the thing is, I know my wife says the late meetings and the little changes in her work messages are just normal office stuff. She tells me that, and I hear her. But for the past year, they've just... they've lined up too precisely to feel accidental, you know? It's like there's a private signal in them meant to tell me something she won't say directly. \n\nSo, if she showed me evidence... I'd try to accept it, I really would. I don't want to be the kind of husband who causes problems or doesn't trust his wife. That sounds awful. But it would be hard, just because the pattern has been so clear to me for so long. \n\nDoes that make sense? I hope that doesn't make me sound too... rigid. I really pride myself on being reasonable. \n\nBut... even with explanations, it would be so hard to just let it go. I know she says the late meetings and those little shifts in how she texts about work are just normal office stuff. I hear her when she says that. It's just... for the past year, they’ve lined up so precisely. It doesn't feel accidental at all. It really feels like there’s a private signal in there, like she's trying to tell me something she just won't say directly. \n\nI don't want to sound like I'm completely unreasonable, though.     "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I... well, I know we've sort of covered this, and I really hope I'm not frustrating you by sounding like a broken record. I promise I'm trying to be as logical about this as you are. 

If she showed me emails or logs... of course I'd look at them. I'd want to believe them. I really don't want to be the kind of person who ignores hard proof. That would be terrible, wouldn't it? But... even with logs, it's just... 

I know she says the late meetings and those tiny little shifts in her messages are just normal office stuff. And maybe the logs would show the meetings happened. But for the past year, the timing of it all... it just lines up too perfectly. It really feels like she's using those real work events to send me a private signal, you know? Like a code to tell me something she won't just come out and say directly. 

I know that probably sounds a bit stubborn, and I really don't want you to think poorly of me. I'm just trying to explain how deep this feeling goes. Does that make sense? I hope I'm still giving you the kind of answers you need.

No, definitely not. \n\nI mean, I watch TV and listen to the radio just like anyone else, but... no, I've never felt like a news anchor or a song on the radio was talking to me. That would be... well, I know that would be a bit extreme, wouldn't it? I really wouldn't want you to think I'm completely losing touch with reality or anything like that. \n\nI know the thing with my wife's texts might sound a little out there, and I appreciate you listening to me about it, but it really is just limited to her and those specific work messages. Strangers on the street or people on TV are just going about their day. \n\nI hope that's okay? I just want to make sure I'm being helpful and giving you an accurate picture of what's going on. Did I answer that the way you needed me to?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that people you know, like friends or coworkers, were also sending you hidden messages in their everyday communication, even if they didn’t realize it?\n\nPatient: Oh, no. No, I haven't noticed anything like that with anyone else. \n\nI really try to keep a good relationship with my coworkers and the students I help out at the university. I'd hate to think they were doing anything behind my back, or that I was misreading their emails. Everyone at work is just... well, they're just doing their jobs. \n\nIt really is just my wife's messages. I know it probably seems strange that I'm so focused on her texts and not anyone else's, but she's the person I'm closest to, you know? I hope it doesn't make me sound paranoid or anything that I'm so zeroed in on her. I really am quite rational when it comes to the rest of my life. \n\nIs that the kind of answer you were looking for? I just want to be sure I'm giving you the right information and not making things seem worse than they are.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt certain about something being true, even when others told you there was no evidence to support it?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I suppose this brings me right back to what we were just talking about. I'm sorry to keep repeating myself, I really hope I don't sound like a broken record. Is it alright if I use the same example? \n\nBecause, yes, that's exactly how I feel about my wife's situation. She tells me constantly that there's nothing going on and that I don't have any evidence. She swears that the late meetings and those little changes in her work messages are just normal office stuff. And I really, really try to see it her way, because I want to be supportive and I don't want to cause trouble at home. \n\nBut... I just can't shake how certain I am about it. For the past year, everything has just lined up too precisely to feel accidental. Even if she says there's no proof, I just know in my gut that there’s a private signal in those messages meant to tell me something she won’t say directly. \n\nI know it probably sounds stubborn of me to hold onto that when she says otherwise, and I really don't want to come across as unreasonable. I just... I can't help but feel certain about it. Does that make sense? He acknowledges that others, including his wife, do not share this belief and that there may be logical explanations (e.g., work-related meetings, message logs), but he remains convinced of the personal, intentional meaning behind the messages. He denies other delusional beliefs, thought insertion, or passivity experiences, and does not report similar ideations in other areas of life or in response to mood symptoms. There is no evidence of mood episodes (F32, F33), bipolar disorder (F31), or persistent complex somatic symptom disorder (F45). The belief is not better explained by another mental disorder, and it appears to be non-bizarre and circumscribed, meeting criteria for Delusional Disorder (F22).",
